@@135915421 <h> The NDP has an antisemitism problem <p> On Sunday , after his runaway victory in the federal New Democratic Party 's leadership race , Avi Lewis linked his own anti-Zionist views to an ancestor 's opposition to the creation of a Jewish state in the Middle East . <p> " My great-grandfather was a leader in the Russian anti-Zionist Jewish Labour Bund , " said Mr. Lewis , who noted that it was founded in 1897 , five weeks after the end of the First Zionist Congress . " So , as long as there 's been formal pro-Israel Zionist organizations , there has been a tradition that has disagreed with that within the Jewish community . " <p> Except , there is a world of difference between describing oneself as an anti-Zionist in 1897 and being one today , more than two years after the Hamas attack on Israel that unleashed the war in Gaza and the global surge in antisemitism that has left Jews everywhere , including in Canada , living in fear . <p> To be an anti-Zionist in 2026 is to deny Israel 's right @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the discourse of left-wing parties in Western countries , fuelling political polarization and violence against Jews unseen since the Holocaust . Left-wing denunciations of Israel 's conduct in Gaza , legitimate on their own , are now routinely laced with antisemitic tropes and dog whistles that serve to encourage anti-Israel extremists . <p> This phenomenon was on disgraceful display at the NDP leadership convention in Winnipeg , where Mr. Lewis delivered his victory speech as keffiyeh-clad supporters joined him on stage and someone behind him waved a massive Palestinian flag . This latter gesture was extremely inappropriate , but it did serve to underscore where the party base now sits . <p> The NDP , once a flag-bearer of the mainstream Canadian left , is now a party of the far left . It threatens to do here what France Unbowed , Spain 's Podemos and Sumar and Germany 's Die Linke have done to politics in their respective countries by envenoming political debate and spewing antagonism . <p> In Europe , the far left and far right feed off each other . For both , conflict is the point @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ inspiration from the European far left , spurning the party 's traditional working-class base in favour of radical identity politics . <p> The new NDP president , Niall Ricardo , greeted last fall 's ceasefire in Gaza , which included a plan to prevent Hamas from rebuilding its military capability , by posting : " The only entity that needs deradicalization and disarmament is the one committing genocide : Israel . Zionism is the sin , genocide is the crime . " <p> If such language is bad enough from a rank-and-file member , one expects party leaders to weigh their words more carefully . Manitoba NDP Premier Wab Kinew spectacularly failed this test when , in a speech to the NDP convention , he propagated an ugly conspiracy theory about the war in Iran that bears more than a whiff of antisemitism . <p> " Let the Epstein class fight the Epstein war , " Mr. Kinew said , implying not only that U.S. President Donald Trump launched the war to divert attention from his past links to the sexual predator Jeffrey Epstein , but that there exists such @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ elites who pull the strings in Washington . B'nai Brith Canada called Mr. Kinew 's comment " an invocation of a loaded and poisonous antisemitic dog whistle " and called on the NDP Premier to apologize . <p> Mr. Kinew , who appears to harbour federal ambitions , is a skilled politician who knows how to read an audience . The crowd in Winnipeg lapped up his words . But his remark risks giving licence to the most radical elements in the NDP . <p> Rima Hassan , a France Unbowed member of the European Parliament , was barred from entering Canada on the weekend after the Centre for Israel and Jewish Affairs raised concerns about her anti-Israel views and " glorification of violence , terrorism and antisemitism . " <p> Ms. Hassan , who was scheduled to speak at two events in Montreal and meet with representatives of the NDP and far-left Qu ? bec Solidaire , caused a political storm in France in February after far-left and far-right protesters violently clashed outside an event she headlined in Lyon , leading to the death of a far-right militant . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ follows . <p> Yet , the NDP co-signed a statement with France Unbowed denouncing the decision by the Canada Border Services Agency to cancel Ms. Hassan 's electronic travel authorization after discovering that she had omitted information on her application about being previously denied entry to Israel and facing accusations in France of glorifying terrorism . <p> By linking up with France Unbowed to defend Ms. Hassan , the NDP showed where it thinks its future lies . That is very worrying , indeed .

@@135944205 <p> Members of Canada 's New Democratic Party are meeting this weekend to choose a new party leader , with a decision slated for Sunday . And for the first time since the 1970s , a Jewish candidate has a strong chance of being elected . <p> Avi Lewis could follow in the footsteps of his late grandfather David Lewis , who led the NDP shortly after Avi was born . David Lewis remains the first Jewish Canadian in history to lead a national political party . ( Annamie Paul led the Green Party from 2020-2021 . ) <p> But a lot has changed since his grandfather 's reign . The NDP was decimated in the 2025 election , winning only seven seats and losing official party status . As they hope to rebuild and expand their coalition , some party members are worried about the younger Lewis 's criticism of Israel and his anti-Zionist beliefs ; he is a member of the anti-Zionist organization Independent Jewish Voices Canada and his roster of prominent endorsers includes many activists with similar views . <p> Lewis 's closest rival is widely seen @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . @@135944206 <h> Everything you need to know about the vandalism of a not-quite-Jewish British bakery <p> Vandals targeted a new location of Gail 's , a prominent British cafe chain with some tangential Israeli connections . ( Photo courtesy combatantisemitism.org ) <p> Earlier this month , British vandals defaced a new location of Gail 's , a bakery chain with 170 locations across the U.K. They smashed the store 's windows , splattered it with red paint , and left pro-Palestinian and anti-Zionist messages on its doors . One such message : " F*** Bain Capital . " It refererred to an investment firm that manages USD$215 billion in global assets , including investments in Israeli security companies and , in some other far corner of the company 's wide reach , the Gail 's brand . <p> Gail 's was also founded by an Israeli baker in the 1990s . So even though the business is not exactly Jewish , it is Jewish enough by vandals ' standards -- and this new location happened to be opening a few blocks away from a popular Palestinian cafe . After the week @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the acts of hatred in The Guardian , describing Gail 's opening as an act of " aggression " towards its Palestinian neighbour . ( The Guardian has since redacted that phrase and others ; the piece remains online . ) <p> At The CJN , opinion editor Phoebe Maltz Bovy had reached out to prominent British Jewish writer Hadley Freeman to discuss Woody Allen 's new novel . But then the Gail 's controversy came up , and they pivoted . This week , Freeman dissects the issue and explains how British progressive movements have evolved over the years -- and , yes , they 'll discuss Woody Allen , too . <p> So let me get this straight:1 . Petty activism against a Palestinian-owned cafe is bad ( agreed ! ) 2 . @@135944207 <h> Letter from Israel at wartime : Life between sirens <p> Screenshot of an app that shows how long people have been in a bomb shelter during the latest attack from Iran , March 26 , 2026 . <p> Nearly a month into the war with Iran , it 's safe to say that much of what we have been experiencing in Israel has been unprecedented . <p> It 's the first fully joint American Israeli military operation . It 's the most audacious effort yet to wipe out the Iranian leadership , foment regime change in the Islamic republic and reshape the region . And it 's the most devastating barrage of missile attacks Israel has ever endured , both in scope ( 110+ air raid sirens and counting in our area of central Israel alone , totaling nearly 30 hours of shelter time , ( as of March 26 ) and type ( the introduction of cluster munitions that make each missile much deadlier ) . <p> As surreal as it may seem to outsiders , we have already somewhat normalized a twisted reality of occasional suicide bombings @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . We 've largely accepted that this is the cost of doing business in the Middle East . But as each round of conflict has intensified and escalated , previously unthinkable scenarios have become routine . <p> Oddly , it was an internal divide that most recently triggered a sense of the ground shifting beneath our feet . Back in 2023 , hundreds of thousands of Israelis took to the streets to protest the government 's attempted judicial overhaul and assault on Israeli democracy . At the time , that seemed unreal . <p> Then came Oct. 7 , and everything changed . As we tried to make sense of that deep trauma , there was at least a glimmer of hope that we had hit rock bottom and could now begin to rebuild . But since then , the punches just keep coming . There was the anguish of the prolonged hostage crisis , the death and devastation to Palestinians ( and our own soldiers ) in Gaza , a spike in Israel 's international isolation and in global antisemitism , an expansion of the missile attacks from Gaza @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and then , finally , Iran as well . <p> Last summer 's 12-day war with Iran upped the ante even further , as the country was effectively shut down with several dozen Israelis killed in strikes and heavy damage caused to military bases , strategic sites and residential areas . We had hoped that unprecedented conflict would buy us an extended spell of quiet . And yet , here we are again , less than a year later , back in a loop of sirens , uncertainty and destruction . <p> On Feb. 28 , we quickly snapped back into that now-familiar war mode of limiting our movements , studying and working remotely , timing our trips to the shower after sirens and squeezing five of us into our steel-doored , fortified safe room to sleep as well as we can at night . ( Even so , we have had perhaps just a handful of nights of uninterrupted sleep so far . ) <p> For many , the frightening first week of persistent missile barrages largely halted day-to-day life . But slowly , the unprecedented has once again @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ siren , go to sleep to another , and watch the news flicker all day long with updates about the various attacks in between . Somehow , we still work , study , go shopping , take a walk and carry on in this new " normal . " <p> It is a daily grind that exposes vulnerability , tests resolve and builds resilience all at once . Others are not as fortunate as we are to have a safe room in their own home and are forced to rush downstairs to a shared or public shelter several times a day -- and night -- often with small children . This has spurred many to relocate to relatives who have more convenient accommodation . Those with no shelter at all simply cover their children and pray . <p> These shelters and underground parking lots have become communal gathering points in turbulent times , often converted into makeshift camp grounds , synagogues and yoga studios . Schools and kindergartens have shut their doors to students -- forcing all three of my kids back to Covid-era remote learning -- but opened them @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oldest daughter , who never properly finished Grade 7 last June because the war period slid into summer vacation , keenly noted that they have n't really returned from this year 's Purim break in early March either , since this war has now run into their Passover break . <p> Technology has made things a bit easier . In addition to the traditional air raid sirens providing 90 seconds to find cover from missiles from Lebanon , various siren apps now also usually provide an additional 10-minute warning after detecting that missiles from Iran are heading your way . It 's quite a spectacle to witness dozens of ring tones simultaneously going off and then see a playground of parents and children instantly disperse . <p> With these sirens and cellphone alerts blaring warnings every few hours , most tend to stay close to home . You think twice before taking to a highway , where you are most vulnerable and have nowhere to hide . The heightened tension might explain why already notoriously aggressive Israeli drivers have been speeding more than ever . It sparked a wartime public @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ scare myself last week when one such driver brazenly whizzed through a red light as I was passing through an intersection . <p> It is only when speaking on video calls to colleagues overseas and casually mentioning the need to leave a meeting to take shelter from a missile , that you realize how one person 's routine is another 's version of insanity . <p> Before Oct. 7 , 2023 , our safe room mostly served as a home office . But since that day , it morphed into the family slumber room with my three daughters sleeping on mattresses on the floor to the sounds of distant explosions outside . As the sirens slowed and eventually ceased ( for a time ) , the older two retreated to their bedrooms , but my youngest never left , claiming the room as her own . <p> Now we 're all back in that room again , where it serves as our hybrid office , classroom , playroom and bedroom for five , as we wait out yet another war that feels like simply the latest in a lifestyle defined @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is a **25;1329;TOOLONG journalist , a former longtime AP correspondent and author of Zaidy 's Band , The Untold Stories of a Jewish Band of Brothers in World War II.You can follow him on Twitter , and read a selection of his work at aronheller.com <p> *** <p> What do you think ? The CJN welcomes your feedback with a letter to the editor . @@135944208 <h> Mark Oppenheimer on Judy Blume 's underappreciated role in the Jewish literary canon <p> Mark Oppenheimer ( left ) is the author of ' Judy Blume : A Life ' , a new biography of the famous Jewish author , out Mar. 10 , 2026 . <p> Judy Blume , the acclaimed author of young people 's novels , saw resurged interest in her work in 2023 . One of her most famous books , Are You There God ? It 's Me , Margaret , hit the big screen as an acclaimed feature-length film ; that same year , Amazon released a documentary about her , Judy Blume Forever . Meanwhile , Mark Oppenheimer -- a writer , podcaster , editor and teacher at the John C. Danforth Center on Religion and Politics -- was asked by the author herself to write her biography . <p> Months after the Blume bonanza , Oct. 7 happened . And while this has little bearing on the public 's appreciation of Blume work , The CJN 's opinion editor , Phoebe Maltz Bovy , got to wondering : would the world @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ openly Jewish characters ) if her movies were slated for 2024 instead ? <p> It 's one of many questions Bovy asks Oppenheimer on this week 's episode of The Jewish Angle , which hones in on Blume 's role in the broader Jewish literary canon . Despite selling around 90 million copies of her books , and even after the revived interest in her work , Blume is rarely granted the same literary standing as her Jewish male contemporaries . @@135944209 <h> Opinion : Stop treating antisemitism as a punishment for Jewish misbehaviour <p> One of my kids was attending a Purim event in downtown Toronto . My husband was going to pick her up from the event , and I said to him something like , you two should watch out , what with attacks on Jewish places in Toronto and elsewhere . I said this in the spirit of a mix of anxiety and resignation , but also with some hopeful part of myself thinking , it 's just that I literally work at a Jewish news outlet , I have read too many ' rise of antisemitism ' headlines , I am primed to have these concerns . <p> Also , a superstitious part of me wondered if simply expressing an anxiety would prevent the thing from happening . <p> If I did superstitiously prevent the anti-Jewish violence I feared , it was only in the narrowest sense of , the specific event my kid was at was not attacked . Later that evening , someone shot a gun into another Toronto-area Jewish institution that had just wrapped @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is dangerous to be publicly Jewish , or really , Jewish at all , is , regrettably , based on fact . I also watch , from Toronto , as my home country , the United States , does a great big left-right alliance over Jews and Israel basically being the worst , with podcasts and posts aplenty offering up Epstein-Mossad conspiracy theories to ever more receptive audiences . <p> As someone with rather clear stakes in wanting antisemitism to cease , I am , I realize , the target audience for takes on how to get rid of it . But I have come to believe that Jews do not have control over this . Obviously , it is in our interests for antisemitism to cease . But also , should-be-obviously , bigotry is not a lesson the hated group must internalize , so as to mend their ways . There are nice Jews and less-nice Jews , good and bad things about Israel . All of this matters to us , as Jews . Exceedingly little of it matters in terms of impacting antisemitism . <p> Plenty of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ professor Ari Y. Kelman , or pundit Matthew Yglesias -- have picked up on the fact that a great big apparatus exists for fighting antisemitism , yet antisemitism has gone nowhere . If anything , it has been on the rise for the last decade , with the most visible surge following Oct. 7 , 2023 . The apparent ineffectual status of the anti-antisemitism movement , its institutions , its memes , has inspired two main , and mutually-exclusive , internal criticisms : <p> The first , and most familiar , goes as follows : Antisemitism is a major problem for us , as Jews ... and a problem whose source is Israel oppressing Palestinians . There are subtypes to this interpretation . One is that Israel needs to change its ways . Another is that Israel needs to cease existing as a Jewish state . Another still is that diaspora Jews need to shout from the rooftops about how terrible they think Israel is , and that until that day comes , they 're getting what 's coming to them . In any case , adherents believe that anti-antisemitism @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The second , newer , less familiar critique is the flip of the previous : It is anti-Zionism , not antisemitism , that needs fighting these days , " anti-Zionism " defined as hating Jews and giving Israel as a pretext . ( One can get into semantic weeds , asking how this is not simply one manifestation of antisemitism . ) Per exponents of this theory , most notably Adam Louis-Klein , the problem is not anything Israel is doing , but rather that anti-Zionist bigots impose a uniquely uncharitable interpretation on everything Israel does , and project their grievances onto Jews globally . Mainstream anti-antisemitism organizing has , in this view , misdirected its energies towards determining what constitutes pure antisemitism as versus mere anti-Zionism , when it is anti-Zionism that 's putting Jews in danger . <p> What these have in common is an underlying conviction , namely that Jews ourselves are in a position to move the needle where anti-Jewish hatred is concerned . If only Israel became single-state Israel-Palestine or diaspora Jews rejected Israel ( critique 1 ) , or if only we began @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the anti-Jewish hatred would stop . <p> The problem we 're actually looking at here is not that the existing mainstream movement against antisemitism has its priorities or terminology wrong . Rather , it 's that all parties -- the anti-antisemitism activists and their critics -- are under the mistaken impression that it matters what Jews ourselves are doing . <p> It 's time to look at the bigger-picture question of how we even came to imagine a minority group 's choices determine how broader society chooses to scapegoat it and at which times . One does not generally see a wave of anti-whichever-group hatred and think , well , what have members of that group been up to lately ? So why do that here ? <p> What I 'm getting at here is not merely that victim-blaming is wrong . It 's that it is illogical in the case of antisemitism , which serves various functions in society , and is not in fact best understood as a response to Jews ' own behaviour , observed holistically and impartially . Antisemitism does not rise when The Jews @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at most a selective , negative response to whatever anyone Jewish has done that one might find objectionable , paired with an endless ability to ignore anything Jews are up to that one might see as positive . <p> Case in point : Don Moynihan , an academic and popular Bluesky account , posted the following , shortly after the United States and Israel attacked Iran : " The Iran war is going to fuel a new level of anti-semitism . " Maybe this was meant as a comment on Donald Trump positioning himself as the anti-antisemitism president , or maybe it was intended as prophecy , or maybe something else entirely . <p> I have no doubt that antisemites will continue to use geopolitics as a pretext . I see it on my own social media feeds . But consider that most American Jews vote Democrat , and that Trump was not American Jews ' preferred presidential candidate , and that Trump won the popular vote . Much of even American Jewish organizations ' ( which can be to the right of the population itself ) ' support ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , This thing that is happening regardless , here 's hoping it goes well . Ordinary American Jews are counting down the minutes until Trump is out of office , something I can not imagine is lost on Trump himself . <p> If you oppose an interventionist American foreign policy and chummy relations between Benjamin Netanyahu and the U.S. president , you should be favourably disposed to a Democratic voting bloc . Funny how this is not how it works out ! <p> By all means , Jews should weigh in on communal organizations ' rhetoric and priorities . Absolutely , we should speak out about the Israeli government 's military choices , the length of the Gaza war , the number of Palestinians killed , and the likelihood that Netanyahu rather than the Israeli people benefitted from how things played out . What I object to is the framing of these critiques as a strategy for mitigating , reducing , or eliminating antisemitism . The best we can hope for is a calm society where people do not attack or harass those they hate . A make-them-like-us campaign is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Author <p> Phoebe is the opinion editor for The Canadian Jewish News and a contributor editor of The CJN 's Scribe Quarterly print magazine . She is also a contributor columnist for the Globe and Mail , co-host of the podcast Feminine Chaos with Kat Rosenfield , and the author of the book The Perils of " Privilege " . Her second book , The Last Straight Woman , will be published by the Signal imprint of Penguin Random House Canada in May 2026 . @@135944222 <p> Former broadcaster , journalist and Canadian political scion Avi Lewis is the new leader of the New Democratic Party . <p> Lewis , 59 , was elected the NDP 's 10th leader on the first ballot in the party 's Saturday vote during its weekend Winnipeg convention . <p> The son of former Ontario NDP leader and Canadian UN ambassador Stephen Lewis , and grandson of the federal party 's second leader David Lewis , Lewis dominated the campaign and handily defeated his rivals , garnering 39,734 of votes , some 56 per cent , almost doubling the votes of his nearest rival , Edmonton-Strathcona MP Heather McPherson , who received 20,899 out of a total 70,930 valid ballots cast . <p> In his victory speech , Lewis again accused Israel of " genocide , " a theme permeating discussions on the floor throughout the weekend , which included calls for housing reforms , workers ' rights , dismantling capitalism , fighting fossil fuel development , appeals for more diversity and equity and more . <p> After his victory was announced , Lewis -- who ran twice federally and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ organizers and party executives on stage to celebrate , including several people sporting keffiyehs as a Palestinian flag waved high above the group while they celebrated new leadership of the 65-year-old political party . <p> Lewis has many hurdles to overcome , with a party that went from being the official Opposition 15 years ago to losing official status following its worst-ever showing in the 2025 election ; currently holding only 6 of 338 House of Commons seats . @@135944227 <h> Bill C-9 the Combatting Hate Act has passed in the House of Commons . What changes now ? <p> Canada 's mainstream Jewish advocacy groups have applauded the government 's passage of Bill C-9 , with a 186-137 vote in the House of Commons on Wednesday night , March 25 . It now goes to the Senate for further debate and a vote . <p> " For more than two years , Jewish communities across Canada have faced escalating threats , intimidation , and violence . Bill C-9 represents an important and necessary step to strengthen tools for law enforcement and prosecutors to protect targeted communities and hold criminals accountable , " said a joint statement , signed by the Centre for Israel and Jewish Affairs , the Alliance of Canadians Combatting Antisemitism , B'nai Brith Canada , Canadian Women Against Antisemitism , Canadian Jewish Law Association and the Friends of Simon Wiesenthal Center . <p> But they 're also urging lawmakers to go even further to tackle Canada 's antisemitism crisis . <p> " While C-9 is a significant step forward , it is not the end of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We must also ensure robust , consistent enforcement of existing laws , improve transparency in prosecutorial decisions , enhance support for community security , and tackle the drivers of radicalization in Canada : unchecked antisemitism , religiously and ideologically motivated extremism and violent anti-Israel activism . " <p> Intimidation and obstruction offences designed to protect access to places of worship and community institutions . ( A new law covering this is different from the bylaws creating so-called " bubble " zones , which have been adopted by some cities in Canada . ) <p> A new hate crime offence that gets tacked on to the charge list when crimes are motivated by hatred , which Ottawa believes will mean stronger penalties . <p> A new offence targeting the wilful promotion of hatred through public display of hate symbols . ( such as the Nazi Hakenkreutz ) . <p> An amended definition of hate which puts into the Criminal Code the widely used hate speech decision from the Supreme Court in 1990 , known as the Keegstra case . <p> " The many recent examples of public displays of support for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ other banned terrorist entities make it clear that Parliamentarians must strengthen Canada 's anti-terror laws as a matter of national security , " said the statement released by the five Jewish groups . <p> Anthony Housefather , the Liberal MP for Mount Royal , has been pushing for this bill . <p> Roman Baber , the Conservative MP for York Centre , led the Opposition campaign against it in the House of Commons . <p> The two Jewish politicians from opposite sides of the aisle spoke to The CJN 's North Star podcast this week to explain their clashing views . We 've broken down the main issues for you in an easy to read format . <p> Religious freedom vs religious leaders preaching hate <p> The bill was held up for months in committee by a Conservative filibuster as MPs debated the removal of an exemption for religious speech from the Criminal Code . <p> Housefather : <p> This is where the Conservative disinformation campaign has kicked into really high gear . The Conservative Party was never supporting this Bill . And they made that incredibly clear at the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ way that the Liberal members of the committee could get the Bill passed , is with the support of the Bloc Quebecois . <p> And if you remember , all of this started by Adil Charkaoui preaching in Montreal . Preaching hateful things , in a public space , not in a mosque , in a public rally , preaching hatred . About Jews . And he was never prosecuted . He was an Imam . <p> Everybody thought he should have been arrested and charged . He was not arrested and charged . At the time , CIJA came out with a position saying this religious defence should be removed . <p> I supported CIJA in that , so , contrary to the Conservatives making the claim that no Liberal member had ever been on the record saying anything about this , you can go back to The CJN issues from two years ago where I was on the record saying that this should be removed . <p> It 's a defence that has never successfully been used in Canadian history . It 's basically saying that if somebody @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you 've risen to that really high level , which the Supreme Court set out in the Keegstra case , to be wilfully promoting hate against an identifiable group , which today is mostly Jews , you could then ground your defence on a reasonable religious belief that what you said is based on your religion . Removing a defence to people who have been wilfully charged with hate speech is not changing anything because nobody has ever used it successfully . <p> And as a result of the fact that we wanted the Bloc certainly to vote with us , we voted in favour of it when the Bloc proposed it . I would have voted in favour of it , for sure , anyways . <p> And what is also in the final iteration of Bill C-9 is a " for greater clarity clause " . <p> Because the Conservatives again have wilfully promoted misinformation . They 've gone across the country telling people " You can no longer read from the Bible , the Quran , or the Torah . " It 's absolutely bull . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not change anyone 's ability to practise their religion or preach their religion . <p> In any case , we put a " for greater certainty clause " into the Bill to say : for greater certainty , nothing in this Bill essentially stops people from speaking or practicing their religion , as long as they 're not wilfully promoting hate . <p> Melissa Lantsman ... told everybody she was going to call every rabbi to come out against this Bill . And so ultra-Orthodox rabbis , just like many other faith leaders who the Conservatives contacted and told , " You 'll never be allowed to preach from the Torah or the Bible or the Qur'an or whatever other religious text " do n't know . <p> They do n't have the legal background to understand or read the bill . @@135944228 <h> Victoria Police to withdraw traffic support for pro-Palestine protests after 126 weeks <p> The Victoria Police Department announced at a press conference on march 25 , 2026 , that effective April 4 , 2026 , they will adjust their operational approach to Rally for Palestine marches . ( credit : VicPD Facebook ) <p> The Victoria Police Department announced this week that after April 4 it will no longer supply traffic control and other police resources to Palestine marches , which have been a Saturday fixture in the city 's downtown for the past 126 weeks , due to officer fatigue and the need to allocate resources elsewhere . <p> At a March 25 press conference , VicPD representatives said the marches were causing an " unsustainable strain " on the department and urged protesters not to march on the streets . The department argued the change was necessary to protect the health and well-being of police officers , while maintaining the right to peaceful assembly and ensuring the department can continue to address public safety throughout the city . <p> " We recognize the passion and commitment of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Constable Fiona Wilson . " This decision is not a judgment on the merits of their cause , but a necessary step to ensure sustainable policing and protect the long-term health of our officers . " <p> " For over two years , we have supported public safety during these demonstrations . However , maintaining this level of continuous , intensive deployment is no longer sustainable . Effective April 4 , VicPD will no longer provide traffic control or related police resources for the march . " <p> Every Saturday afternoon since October 2023 , protests have begun at the British Columbia Parliament Buildings and moved onto the downtown streets before returning to their starting point . From April 4 onwards , VicPD is strongly encouraging organizers and participants to remain on the Legislature lawn , where the environment is safer and more controlled , and on sidewalks and other designated public spaces to help reduce safety risks . <p> At the next rally , scheduled for March 28 , police officers will be handing out pamphlets to marchers and members of the public that will describe the new approach @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more than 10,000 hours of officers ' time have been deployed for the events . The cost for policing the marches , funded by the province , for the past two years is estimated to be $1.2 million . The protests have resulted in eight arrests . <p> " This is the only protest that has continued for 126 consecutive weeks that has reached the point where it 's unsustainable for our officers to maintain , " said Insp . Conor King , who told the media that roughly 100 protesters march every Saturday and that police send four to six officers to each rally . <p> According to Jeff Bray , the CEO of the Downtown Victoria Business Association , the protests have become problematic for stores operating in the area . He told The CJN that the impact on downtown businesses -- while difficult to quantify in dollars -- was not only the actual disruption of traffic during the protests , but also that Victoria police would issue public warnings about traffic disruptions every Saturday in downtown . <p> " This clearly acted as a deterrent for some @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stores , cafes , etc. , " Bray said . <p> " While we all support the right to protest , and as a provincial capital , we expect to have more of this activity than other B.C. cities , over 120 weekly marches that closed off streets and deterred customers from coming downtown have had a sizeable negative impact on businesses . We support Victoria Police 's decision to no longer provide traffic control or related police resources for the street portion of the demonstration . " <p> On June 7 , 2025 , some protesters from the weekly march changed course from their usual route when they learned that Nina Krieger , a Jewish member of the provincial legislative assembly , was hosting a fundraising event at a nearby park . <p> Protestors shouted at Krieger , who , prior to entering politics , was executive director of the Vancouver Holocaust Education Centre , through a fence at the family-friendly event that featured lawn bowling , a barbecue and a colouring zone for children . Leaders of both major political parties in B.C. , the ruling NDP and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ she is Jewish . <p> Nico Slobinsky , the vice-president of the Pacific Region for the Centre for Israel and Jewish Affairs , told The CJN , " While VicPD 's decision to withdraw support from the street portion of the weekly protests is a welcome step , it also highlights what Jewish Victorians have endured for the past two years -- regular disruptions to their daily lives and , in some cases , direct targeting , including the incident involving a Jewish elected official on June 7 . <p> " More must be done to ensure that people can enjoy Victoria 's streets free from harassment or disruptive demonstrations . We call on VicPD to continue to work with our community to increase patrols and presence around our places of worship and community spaces to ensure we can build a safer city for all , " he added . <p> Slobinsky also pointed to an Oct. 7 , 2025 , survey released by Research.co , two years after the Hamas attacks , that found 79 per cent of British Columbians are " very concerned " or " moderately concerned @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The poll also found that 86 per cent of people surveyed in the province were worried by the aggressive behaviour of protesters , and 76 per cent felt that the statements by protesters were antisemitic . Three-in-four British Columbians agreed that Canadian Jews are being unfairly targeted for what was happening in Israel and Gaza , and 48 per cent said they would feel " much less safe " or " somewhat less safe " than two years ago if they were Jewish Canadians . <p> Protests in other parts of the country have put financial strains on police budgets . In the three months following Oct. 7 , 2023 , Vancouver police stated that 80 protests resulted in 1,800 overtime shifts at a cost of $2.5 million . Vancouver police said protests were partially responsible for $1.65 million in additional overtime allocations in its 2026 budget . <p> Earlier this month , Ottawa Police Services said that over 20 per cent of its budget deficit was due to the federal government 's refusal to cover the costs of policing protests related to the conflict in Gaza . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ anti-Israel rally to stick to the main intersection of Bathurst Street and Sheppard Avenue and not demonstrate on residential side streets , in the heavily Jewish neighbourhood . @@135944230 <h> McGill law students referendum on Israel triggers legal challenge and university intervention <p> A student referendum at McGill University 's Faculty of Law on an academic boycott of Israel has set off a cascade of consequences -- including the resignation of the chair of the faculty 's advisory board , a legal challenge and a formal rebuke from the university . <p> The vote , held recently by the Law Students ' Association ( LSA ) , asked members to approve amendments to the student society 's constitution . According to communications from university officials , the proposal centres on embedding support for an academic boycott of Israeli educational institutions into the LSA 's governing framework . <p> While the full text of the motion has not been publicly released in detail by the LSA , multiple sources -- including a resignation letter from a senior faculty advisor , and a letter from the president of the university -- indicate that it aligns with boycott , divestment and sanctions ( BDS ) -style measures . These typically include commitments to avoid institutional partnerships with Israeli universities , refrain from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with Israeli academic bodies . <p> In a message sent to law students following the vote , dean Tina Piper confirmed that the referendum " garnered a plurality of votes in favour , " meaning it received more support than any alternative option but not necessarily an outright majority of all ballots cast . <p> She acknowledged the divisive impact of the result , writing that it " will inevitably be experienced differently across our community , " and said processes are now underway to determine the amendments ' formal status and implications . <p> Piper reiterated that the university views the proposed changes as " discriminatory in effect and could adversely affect the safety and inclusion of students . " She emphasized that , even if adopted , the amendments would apply only to the student association 's internal constitution and " have no effect on the operation of the Faculty of Law . " <p> The referendum appears to have been introduced in the context of ongoing campus debates since the October 2023 Hamas attacks on Israel and the subsequent war in Gaza , which have prompted a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ institutional boycotts or divestment measures tied to Israel . <p> At McGill , those tensions have played out over the past two academic years through violent protests , property vandalism , and targeting of Jewish and Israeli students . <p> The university 's response to the law faculty vote has been unusually direct . <p> In a letter to alumni , McGill president Deep Saini said the amendments are " inoperative and non-receivable , " arguing they fall outside the LSA 's authority and conflict with university policy . <p> He said the amendments constitute a breach of the Memorandum of Agreement governing the LSA 's relationship with the university . Under that agreement , student associations must operate in a manner consistent with their own constitutions and with McGill policies , including those related to discrimination and harassment . <p> If the amendments are formally adopted , Saini said , McGill will place the LSA in default of that agreement , triggering a 30-day window for the student association to reverse the changes . If it does not , the LSA could lose key privileges , including the use @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the ability to distribute student publications . <p> The dispute is also moving through the courts . Saini said a law student has launched legal proceedings challenging the amendments , with McGill supporting the claim that they are discriminatory . <p> The student 's application for an interlocutory injunction preventing implementation of the referendum vote policy was rejected by a Quebec Court Friday afternoon , March 27 . Along with stating that the plaintiff had not demonstrated the " urgent need " required for injunctive relief , Judge Luc Morin noted the referendum wording is " muddled . " He stated the plaintiff 's argument that the case is discriminatory requires a more thorough debate than can be had " at this provisional stage . " <p> The situation escalated further on March 22 , when Jonathan Amiel , chair of the faculty 's advisory board and a university donor , resigned from both that role and his position as a course lecturer , while withdrawing financial support from the faculty . <p> In his resignation letter , Amiel wrote that he was " no longer willing or able to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " and would withdraw " all current and future pledges and donations . " <p> He said the referendum " endorsed an academic boycott of Israeli educational institutions " and argued it reflects " a broader and sustained failure of institutional leadership . " <p> Amiel described what he called an " escalating pattern of hostility toward Jewish students , faculty , and alumni , " adding that " being Jewish , identifying as a Zionist , or maintaining any association with the State of Israel carries professional and personal risk . " <p> He also cited incidents he said included classroom disruptions , obstruction of access to campus spaces and the normalization of rhetoric that contravenes university policy . <p> At issue in the McGill case is not only the substance of the referendum , but the limits of student governance . Although the LSA operates as an independent student body , it remains bound by contractual obligations with the university -- an arrangement that allows McGill to intervene when policies are deemed to violate institutional standards . <p> In Amiel 's resignation letter , he warned that failure @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ declining donor support and weakened alumni engagement . " An institution that can not ensure the safety , inclusion , and dignity of its own community can not credibly sustain its claim to excellence , " he wrote . <p> Jamie Fabian , a recent McGill law graduate and C ? te Saint-Luc city councillor , told The CJN the referendum reflects deeper issues within the faculty 's intellectual climate . " I think this needs to serve as a wake-up call to McGill Law admissions and to the administration generally , " he said , arguing that during his time as a student , " there 's certainly a dominant and prevailing discourse " around social issues , where " there 's an expected opinion that a student should have . " Students who diverge from that consensus , he added , can face social consequences : " If you deviate from that in any way , there 's a loud contingent of students that will seek to isolate you ... That 's not healthy for academic freedom . " <p> Fabian said the practical effects of the referendum @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ international academic programs involving Israeli institutions . While noting that the measure is not binding on the faculty itself , he emphasized its symbolic weight . " The bulk of the consequences are more so the message it 's sending about academic freedom and students ' ability to engage , " he said . Fabian added that , based on conversations with fellow alumni across the legal field , the vote risks damaging the McGill Law 's reputation . <p> McGill Law alumnus Toby Moneit says the implications of this motion extends beyond the walls of the university . " When I was a student at the Faculty of Law , we learned about law , systems of law and the interplays between them , " she wrote in a message to The CJN . <p> " Students came from and went to varied backgrounds . Some ended up in law firms . Others did clerkships around the world -- Quebec Court of Appeal , Supreme Court of Canada , Rwanda international tribunal , even the Israeli Supreme Court . The law student association was about law students , representing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Now it seems to be completely ideological . " <p> She added , " If our law faculties are ideologically captured , our democracy is threatened . " <p> March 30 , 2026 : The story was updated to reflect the outcome of the legal challenge . <p> With files from Joel Ceausu <h> Author <p> Mitch is The CJN 's campus and education reporter based in Toronto , Ont . @@135948207 <h> ' Integrate or perish ' <p> Retired former Jamaican Prime Minister P.J. Patterson has warned the 15-member Caribbean bloc , CARICOM , that it must " integrate or perish " in the face of mounting threats to international law , shifting political dynamics , and economic pressures affecting the region . <p> Speaking at the Norman Manley Law School during the Council of Legal Education 's Distinguished Lecture Series 2026 yesterday evening , Patterson said the regional body risks failure without stronger systems to enforce its decisions . <p> " CARICOM will flounder unless there is an effective machine and binding mechanisms to implement and enforce the solemn decisions of heads and ministerial organs , " he said . <p> Delivering a lecture titled ' Constitutional Renewal , Caribbean Integration and the Rule of Law : Charting the Region 's Next Chapter ' , Patterson argued that the bloc must reposition itself as a force for transformation . <p> " A reignited CARICOM must become a catalyst for change . We need a seismic shift to be more cohesive -- not more divided and supine . We have to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ self-determination in a world that too often marginalises our interests , " he charged . <p> Referencing the legacy of National Hero Norman Manley , after whom the law school is named , Patterson recalled his vision of building " a community that provides a wider field for individual ambition " . <p> For decades , the region has faced criticism for failing to fully realise its goals of integration , including trade , free movement , and coordinated policy action . While there have been successes -- particularly in sports such as track and field at the Carifta level -- long-standing concerns remain . Cricket , once a unifying force , has also faced criticism , with allegations of insularity , especially from smaller territories . Free trade agreements and free movement arrangements have likewise been inconsistently applied . <p> Patterson insisted that the next phase of CARICOM must be centred on tangible benefits for Caribbean people . <p> " The next chapter of our community must achieve that wider ambition , centred on the Caribbean people . CARICOM must make a difference in the daily quality of life @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and education , who should be able to operate regionally through a final protocol on contingent rights ; to our entrepreneurs , who should function in a fully operational single market ; and to litigants , who should access their final court of justice without having to obtain a visa , " he said . <p> A long-standing advocate for replacing the United Kingdom-based Privy Council , Patterson again called for full regional adoption of the Caribbean Court of Justice as the final appellate court , more than 20 years after its establishment . <p> He also stressed the need for a stronger public information framework to help citizens better understand and engage with CARICOM 's work , particularly in an era dominated by social media . <p> " CARICOM will not matter to the average citizen who has little or no information as to what is available through the community without an information arm , adequately staffed and equipped to disseminate its work and achievements , " he said . <p> According to Patterson , the region 's progress will depend on both institutional strength and public commitment . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the pillars already in the ground , and will require both steel and zeal for a sturdy Caribbean architecture on sound foundations , " he said . <p> He called for a sustained regional campaign , invoking Caribbean intellectual George Lamming , to place Caribbean identity at the centre of education . <p> " The time is overdue for the Caribbean to be at the centre of the curriculum at all levels of the region 's education system -- not simply as a matter of geography , but as an organic path in understanding who we are as one people , " Patterson said . <p> Turning to Jamaica 's constitutional reform process , Patterson questioned how the current legislative stalemate would be resolved , noting that deliberations appear to have stalled . <p> He argued that the process must be transparent , with the public kept informed , and raised questions about whether previous bills would be re-tabled or whether a bipartisan consensus would be pursued . He warned that constitutional timelines must be respected . <p> Patterson also pointed to declining voter turnout as a troubling trend , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ legitimacy . <p> He said it was regrettable that Jamaica 's 65th anniversary would pass with the British monarch still as head of state , the Privy Council still serving as the final court of appeal , and a Constitution that remains rooted in an Order in Council , with what he described as a cumbersome amendment process under Section 49 . <p> " It is time to repatriate our Constitution and turn the final page , " he said . <p> Addressing the state of international law , Patterson revisited concerns he first raised in a 2011 lecture , noting that the gap between domestic and international law has widened . <p> " We are engulfed by turbulent waves within our Caribbean Sea by the exercise of military might and proclamations which violate the tenets of international law , " he said . <p> He criticised what he described as global double standards , pointing to the position of the United States on the International Court of Justice . <p> " Yet it has the effrontery to impose visa sanctions on prosecutors for daring to prepare a writ of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Palestinians in Gaza , " he said . <p> He also raised concerns about reports of extrajudicial actions within the region , questioning how such actions could be reconciled with constitutional protections guaranteeing the right to life and due process . <p> The lecture also included reflections on the life and legacy of Norman Manley . <p> Dr Christopher Malcolm , principal of the law school , said the initiative was student-led and emphasised the importance of supporting attorneys in training . <p> Among those in attendance were former Governor General Sir Kenneth Hall and Lady Hall , as well as Marva McDonald-Bishop , president of the Court of Appeal .

@@136000105 <p> My grandparents ' large house was built in the 1960s . Growing up , I adored how during family gatherings its capacious rooms and dark corners served as the perfect venue for a good game of hide-and-seek with my brother and cousins . <p> I now understand that the house represented Bubbie 's and Zadie 's rise from modest beginnings in Toronto 's Kensington Market , where her family owned a kosher poultry shop and his owned a bagel bakery . Its grand staircase and formal d ? cor carried the weight of another time -- an era after the Holocaust when many Jewish families were stepping into a new world of opportunity and security . <p> In September , 2023 , I visited my 95-year-old Bubbie at the house . She had adamantly , even stubbornly , insisted on aging in her home . The furniture on the main floor had been thinned out and an electric stairlift wound up the grand staircase . Bubbie was connected to an oxygen machine and a caregiver was always nearby . Yet Bubbie was still the same person who had embraced @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ So , we shared our latest Netflix binges , played Bananagrams and , in passing , talked about Israeli politics -- unaware that October 7 was around the corner . When I left , Bubbie hugged me tight and whispered , " I love you , darling . " <p> Her funeral a few months later fell on a snowy day . After I helped carry Bubbie 's dark casket through the cemetery , I hugged my mom and said I loved her . <p> We returned to Bubbie 's house for the Shiva , a Jewish mourning ritual . In a quiet moment , I thumbed through a photo album of my grandparents ' 1970 trip to Israel , which was hosted by the Israeli government in appreciation of the fundraising efforts of the Canadians travelling in the group . There they were , in idyllic landscapes and beside landmarks such as the King David Hotel . The family laughed at a photo taken at a private event that showed Bubbie in front of Israel 's first prime minister , David Ben-Gurion , who appears in the background like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ never lived in Israel . More than a place , Israel was an idea . <p> With the house to be put on the market , my mom and her siblings decided to host one last Passover Seder there in 2024 , marking both Jewish liberation from slavery and the end of an era for our family . <p> The evening before Passover , one of our family members sent an e-mail noting that the family was " deeply divided " over the conflict in the Middle East and asking that we refrain from " starting , or engaging in , divisive discussions or arguments . " To be honest , I felt relief knowing there would n't be a pie fight . <p> The gathering was nostalgic and lovely . We reminisced about Bubbie . We went around reading from the Passover Haggadah . In Hebrew , my cousin 's children chanted the four questions -- when the youngest person present asks about the meaning of Passover and the family answers by retelling the story of exodus from Egypt . <p> There was a yellow ribbon on the Seder @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Some family members wore dog tags with the engraving , " Bring Them Home . " <p> We celebrated Jewish liberation , but did not discuss Gaza 's destruction . Recalled plagues , but did not mention Israel 's occupation . Ended with the perennial words " next year in Jerusalem , " but ignored the Nakba and the longing of Palestinians ever since to return to their homeland . <p> As it happens , the following year I travelled to Jerusalem . At the city 's edge , an Israeli guide walked me down a steep hill to visit the ruins of Lifta , a Palestinian village whose residents fled violence and were expelled in 1947 and 1948 . My Israeli guide told me how growing up he found the stone houses magical and otherworldly , but he was not told who used to live there . Later in life , it all clicked when he met a Palestinian man who had been expelled from Lifta as a child . Not allowed to live there , he visited his old home every week . <p> This Passover , I @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and about Bubbie , who chose to stay in hers until the end . <p> Bubbie 's house was sold and its objects divvied up . My mom helped me snag Bubbie 's quirky owl-shaped cookie jar , grandfather clock and Shabbat candlesticks , which I light on Friday nights with my husband . Using some money that I inherited from Bubbie , I contacted a Palestinian artist . He shipped me two paintings -- a landscape and a still life . We hung them in our home with help from friends . I do n't know what Bubbie would have thought about that choice . But inheritance -- whether of objects , stories or identity itself -- should n't just be the passive receipt and protection of what has been given . Each generation is responsible for deciding what to do with these legacies . <p> Passover teaches that freedom begins with asking questions about oppression , memory and justice . Perhaps the real challenge in answering is noticing which stories we choose to tell .

@@136046763 <h> Montreal college cancels Shoah commemoration ; vows to reschedule <p> This year marked Vanier College 's 34th annual Holocaust and Genocide Symposium , which over the decades has offered thousands of students lessons and testimonies on genocide , fighting antisemitism and other forms of hatred , and first-hand perspectives on history 's greatest crimes against humanity . <p> Alongside 15 speakers over five days , a commemorative service has traditionally honoured the memory of the six million Jews murdered in the Holocaust , and includes local dignitaries , members of the Israeli , Austrian , and German diplomatic corps , as well as students , survivors and their families . <p> But that did n't happen this year , or at least not the way it was supposed to . <p> Vanier , one of three English public colleges in Montreal , cancelled the annual commemorative service , citing security concerns based on information the school received , but would not specify . <p> Days before it was slated to take place , the March 25 commemoration , traditionally a sombre affair , held in a small conference room @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ holds a significant place in Vanier 's institutional history , and " brings together a number of external guests and dignitaries , students , staff , and members of the general public . In light of the current volatile geopolitical climate and given the high-profile nature of the dignitaries scheduled to attend , we have made the difficult decision to cancel the commemoration as originally planned . " <p> Vanier 's director-general Benoit Morin defended the decision to cancel over security concerns , while acknowledging the symbolic impact on the Jewish community . He said with participants , including elected officials and community stakeholders , this year 's event had been planned before the most recent outbreak of war in Iran . In light of that , administrators decided to explore alternatives and hold the commemoration under safer parameters . <p> " The focus on security , " he told The CJN , contributed to " a blind spot " with respect to the potential symbolic impact it could have on Holocaust survivors and the Jewish community , " and for that miscommunication , we apologize . " <p> Not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ echoed the sentiment of frustration expressed by Warsaw-born Holocaust survivor and MHM board member Eva Kuper who was scheduled to speak , and who told the Montreal Gazette , " I do n't think that when you 're being bullied by people who have other views that the answer is to kowtow and to cancel . " <p> In a statement , the MHM said " tensions on campuses and conflicts in the Middle East should not be grounds for cancellation of Holocaust commemoration events in Montreal " and instead invited all students to the museum , free of charge , to learn from survivors ' stories . <p> Morin flatly rejected , however , that Vanier governs itself out of fear of extremism or hostility to Jewish-themed events . " The Holocaust symposium is a long-standing tradition ; it is pretty unique for a higher education institution and to my knowledge , the only one of its kind in Quebec . <p> He also rejected the notion that Holocaust survivors who endured Auschwitz are n't safe at Vanier : " That 's a little bit extreme , " he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ relationship with the Jewish community and intend to keep it that way , and we 're committed more than other institutions to promoting important knowledge about Jewish culture , Jewish history , and the symposium is a flagship element in that commitment . " <p> Apart from the cancellation , which brought negative attention to a campus already in the sights of Quebec 's education ministry for reports of a toxic campus environment due to the Gaza-Israel war , the overt security presence required at the week-long symposium highlighted the lived reality for many Jewish or Israel-themed gatherings at Vanier or elsewhere across the Island of Montreal over the past two-plus years . <p> The Holocaust and Genocide Symposium was held in an amphitheatre requiring guests and staff to leave , to rotate attendance with students while respecting venue capacity limits . Security guards sporting tactical vests and earpieces stood watch inside the room and outside in the hallway , checking student IDs , and an event organizer had to visually approve those entering . <p> Three weeks earlier , Vanier 's other long-running symposium -- International Women 's Week @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ evident at the Holocaust series . It included a day-long Palestinian-themed roster , the day 's sole event co-sponsored by both Quebec 's Ministry of Higher Education and Vanier 's teachers ' union , featuring Concordia activists talking about " over-securitization " of campuses , " campus struggle , " and " academic repression . " <p> The cancelled 90-minute Shoah commemoration planned for Wednesday , March 25 , was to include Israeli and German consuls-general , along with survivors , students and interns from Austria 's Gedenkdienst internship program -- which allows Austrian youth to work in Holocaust and Jewish institutions around the world in lieu of military service . <p> After word got out that Vanier was shutting down a Holocaust memorial in the face of a perceived security issue , the story made news across North America , Europe and in Israel . <p> On March 24 , symposium speaker Natasha Pein delivered a talk at Vanier that The CJN attended , on the Soviet roots of anti-Zionism and its masking of modern Jew-hatred . Canada , Pein told some 40 students , is " among the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ crime and incident data with other Western countries . @@136050830 <h> Michael Abrahams Israel 's Palestinian purge prescription <p> On March 30 , 1976 , Israeli police killed six unarmed Palestinian citizens of Israel and injured over 100 others while they were protesting the state 's confiscation of nearly 5,000 acres of Palestinian land in Galilee . Since then , March 30 has been designated Land Day , a day commemorating the collective action and resistance of Palestinians against Israeli domination and oppression . <p> However , this year , on the 50th anniversary of Land Day , Israel 's parliament made a move to further demoralise Palestinians . It passed a law that would make the death penalty the default sentence for Palestinians convicted of deadly terror attacks deemed " acts of terrorism " to " negate the existence of the state of Israel " . Persons found guilty would be executed by hanging within 90 days , with a possible postponement of up to 180 days . The new law passed its third and final reading in the Knesset by 62 votes to 48 , with Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu voting in favour . <p> The legislation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir . Ben-Gvir , who has previous convictions for supporting a terrorist organisation and for inciting racism , was among several lawmakers who wore lapel pins with gold nooses , symbolising the executions ( replacing the yellow-ribbon lapel pins widely worn while Israeli hostages were held in Gaza ) , to the session . He also ostentatiously brandished a bottle of Champagne , which he later poured and shared with supporters , while gleefully declaring , " Soon we will count them one by one " . <h> STARK ILLUSTRATION <p> The new law is a stark illustration of the inequality of the legal systems applying to Palestinians and to Israelis . For instance , Palestinians are tried in military courts , and under the new law , those found guilty of killing Israelis will receive an automatic death penalty , with life imprisonment only available under extreme circumstances . And the conviction rates in these courts are exceedingly high . Of the Palestinians tried for offences committed in the occupied West Bank , 96 to 99 per cent are found guilty , with convictions often @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ no jury or entitlement to pardon . And children are not exempt , as Israel is the only country in the world that systematically prosecutes children in military courts . <p> On the other hand , Israeli settlers in the West Bank are tried in civilian courts in Israel , and Israel has not prosecuted any of its citizens for killing Palestinians in the West Bank since the start of this decade . According to the Israeli rights group , Yesh Din , conviction rates for settlers found guilty of committing crimes against Palestinians in the West Bank ( excluding East Jerusalem ) between 2005 and 2024 were about 3 per cent . <p> And Palestinians in the West Bank are attacked and killed by members of the Israel Defence Forces ( IDF ) and by Jewish settlers with impunity . Over 1,000 Palestinians have been killed there since October 7 , 2023 , including over a dozen since the Iran War began on February 28 , with settler violence being a major contributing factor . On March 15 , just hours after Israeli settlers shot and killed Amir Moatasem @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and stabbed and seriously wounded his father , Moatasem Awda , an undercover Border Police unit in the West Bank opened fire on a car carrying a father , mother and four children . Othman , 7 , who reportedly had special needs and was blind , and Mohammed , 5 , and their parents , Ali Khaled Bani Odeh , 37 , and Waad Othman Bani Odeh , 35 , were killed after all were shot in the head and face . Two other sons , Khaled , 12 , and Mustafa , 8 , were injured by shrapnel . Khaled said that after the shooting , a soldier took him out of the car , beat him and said , " We killed some dogs " . <h> DISCRIMINATORY <p> The law , which specifically targets one ethnic group , is manifestly discriminatory . Legal expert Amichai Cohen of the Israel Democracy Institute confirmed to the Associated Press that Jewish citizens would not face prosecution under the new law . Unsurprisingly , the legislation has been widely condemned , especially by human rights organisations including Amnesty International @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ public outreach director Shai Parnes said , " It 's just going to be another tool in the Israeli toolkit to kill Palestinians . " Even before the vote , Germany , France , Italy , and the United Kingdom issued a joint statement urging Israel to abandon the legislation . <p> The law 's legality is also being questioned . The Association of Civil Rights in Israel announced it had taken the matter to the country 's highest court minutes after the bill was approved , arguing that the measure was " discriminatory by design " and that lawmakers had no legal authority to impose it on Palestinians living in the occupied West Bank , who are not Israeli citizens . <p> The death penalty for murder was outlawed in Israel in 1954 . Only two people have been executed in the country 's 78-year history . Meir Tobianski was executed for treason in 1948 but was exonerated a year later , and Adolf Eichmann , the Nazi architect of the Holocaust , was hanged in 1962 . Now Israel is seeking to make the execution of Palestinians a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the first state since Nazi Germany to codify death as a punishment reserved for one specific ethnic group . Not even apartheid South Africa did that . Over 90 Palestinians have died in custody since October 7 , 2023 , and approximately 9,500 remain detained under harsh conditions , with about half held under administrative detention or labelled " unlawful combatants " , denied trial and unable to defend themselves . Many of them could soon face execution . <p> The Supreme Court must now decide whether to hear the challenge to the bill . Regardless of the outcome , the passage of the law and the celebratory reaction it provoked are sobering testaments to the dehumanisation Palestinians in the occupied territories confront . We should all be outraged . <p> Michael Abrahams is an obstetrician and gynaecologist , social commentator , and human-rights advocate . Send feedback to columns@gleanerjm.com and michabe1999@hotmail.com , or follow him on X , formerly Twitter , @mikeyabrahams

@@136083068 <h> How Pakistan came to play peacemaker in Iran <p> U.S. President Donald Trump said Tuesday that he had agreed to a two-week ceasefire with Iran , less than two hours before his deadline for Tehran to reopen the Strait of Hormuz . <p> Reuters <p> As he declared " total and complete victory " Tuesday in his war against Iran , U.S. President Donald Trump credited two men with making it happen , Pakistan 's Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif , and Asim Munir , head of the country 's armed forces . <p> For weeks , Mr. Sharif and Mr. Munir , who as army chief is the true power in Pakistan , have been acting as intermediaries between Tehran and Washington , leveraging connections to both parties and regional powers such as Saudi Arabia and China to try and secure a ceasefire . <p> In a social media post late Tuesday , Mr. Trump said Pakistan had " requested that I hold off the destructive force being sent tonight to Iran , " and secured an agreement from Iran that it , too , would hold off on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Negotiations on what Mr. Trump described as a " definitive Agreement concerning Longterm PEACE with Iran " are expected to begin in Islamabad on Friday . <p> Beyond staving off Mr. Trump 's threat that " a whole civilization will die tonight " if Iran did not agree to his terms -- which sparked global alarm and calls in the U.S. for Mr. Trump to be immediately removed from office -- Tuesday 's agreement and the coming talks represent a diplomatic coup for Pakistan , thrusting Islamabad into the heart of global politics and potentially ending a war that had threatened to destabilize both the country and the wider region . <p> " Pakistan has a long history in mediating between rival powers , dating back to the Cold War , " said Charles Lyons-Jones , a research fellow at the Sydney-based Lowy Institute and former Australian diplomat in Pakistan . " Just as in brokering detente between the United States and China during the Cold War , Pakistan 's military leadership likely sees a resolution to the U.S.-Iran conflict as firmly in its interests . " <p> U.S. @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Shehbaz Sharif speaks after the official signing of the first phase of the Gaza ceasefire agreement between Israel and Hamas in Sharm El-Sheikh , Egypt , Oct. 13 , 2025 . Like many countries in Asia -- which are particularly dependent on energy and other resources that pass through the Strait of Hormuz -- Pakistan has been suffering severe shortages of both fuel and fertilizer , forcing the government to enact a four-day work week for civil servants and a 10 p.m. curfew for many businesses . <p> Pakistan , while officially a Sunni Muslim country , also has a substantial Shia minority , mostly located along its 900-kilometre border with Iran , which has a Shia majority . The U.S.-Israeli war and the killing of former Iranian supreme leader Ali Khamenei has already led to protests and unrest in Pakistan . <p> As the conflict expanded , there was also the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of its mutual defence pact with Saudi Arabia , which essentially places the Kingdom under Islamabad 's nuclear umbrella . <p> Despite its closeness to Saudi Arabia and the U.S. , and an exchange of missiles in early 2024 , Pakistan has maintained good , " brotherly " ties with Iran , putting it in the rare position of having a connection to all sides of the conflict ( barring Israel , which has already disputed some elements of Tuesday 's peace plan ) . <p> " Pakistan shares very strong relations with both countries and as a result enjoys a fair degree of trust from both the U.S. and Iran , " said Arsla Jawaid , associate director for the Middle East and North Africa at Control Risks , a security and strategic intelligence firm . <p> Pakistan was bolstered in its efforts by China , Mr. Trump confirmed late Tuesday , saying Beijing had helped get Iran to the table . " <p> " And it is , " she added . " For a long time , it has sought a more central role in the Middle East and has finally positioned itself to be seen as a credible and reliable actor . <p> " Being seen as a peacemaker in an escalating conflict that has engulfed many countries around the world will work heavily in Pakistan 's favour in the long term , " Ms. Jawaid said , though she noted Islamabad will struggle to play a role beyond facilitator and mediator " due to the limited leverage it holds over either side . @@136115754 <p> When the news of the U.S.-Iran ceasefire broke , I was reminded of an exchange described by U.S. colonel Harry Summers in 1982 . " You never defeated us on the battlefield , " he said to a former North Vietnamese colonel . " Yes , but we won the war , " was the categorical response . <p> Make no mistake : the deal seals the strategic defeat of the U.S.-Israel alliance in Iran . This war will be remembered as yet another episode of powerful countries falling into the trap of asymmetric warfare , in which the mightiest militaries invariably fail to translate tactical gains into strategic victories . <p> The principles of war , laid out by Carl von Clausewitz in 1812 , make clear that the destruction of enemy forces should have a terminal impact on their will to resist . Asymmetric wars defy this norm of " decisive battle , " and there was no reason to think that Iran would be an exception . A civilization animated by ideological fervour , which has endured centuries of wars of survival , was never going @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of its people 's lives , including thousands of children , in its eight-year war against Iraq in the 1980s always had a tremendous advantage over enemies that crumble under the emotional impact of a few dozen body bags . A regime that in January murdered tens of thousands of its own citizens in a mere 48 hours was not going to be fazed by threats against civilians . <p> Even as the U.S. and Israel have killed much of the Islamic Republic 's political and military leadership , and demolished much of its military capacity , the regime has waged a war of attrition against the global economy by blocking transit through the Strait of Hormuz . In the process , Iran has managed to replenish its budget : it is now earning nearly twice as much from oil sales as before the war , while raking in profits from taxing ships for passage through the Strait . <p> Cargo ships wait in the Gulf , near the Strait of Hormuz , in early March . Even the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz after the ceasefire can not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ To top it all off , the U.S. and Israel have failed to achieve any of their war aims . Even the reopening of the Strait can not be considered a victory , since it was open before the war . Iran 's ballistic-missile capabilities and its enriched uranium supplies remain a problem that will be addressed through diplomacy . <p> Different individuals now lead Iran , but they are no more moderate than their predecessors . The Islamic Republic has been transformed into an outright military dictatorship , with the Ayatollahs providing religious legitimacy to the hardline Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps . <p> The broader regional implications are no more favourable to the U.S. and Israel . The war is bound to lead to a redrawing of the geopolitical map of the Middle East . Ties among the countries most overtly challenging the Western-led global order -- China , Iran , Russia and North Korea -- might be strengthened , and their resolve hardened . <p> At the same time , the Gulf states , which have borne the brunt of Iran 's retaliatory strikes , might start viewing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ effective deterrent and move to diversify their alliances . They may consider aligning with a regional power like Turkey , which already has ties to the Gulf Cooperation Council , or Pakistan , which has a defence treaty with Saudi Arabia and has shown a willingness to share its nuclear know-how with Islamic states . In fact , the likelihood of nuclear proliferation in the Middle East has now grown , as leaders in Iran and elsewhere come to view nuclear weapons as the ultimate insurance policy . <p> Anti-war protestors gather in Habima Square in Tel Aviv last week . Benjamin Netanyahu 's wars in Gaza , Lebanon and Iran have divided the country and tarnished Israel 's standing in the world.Erik Marmor/Getty Images <p> As for Israel , unless it holds Benjamin Netanyahu accountable for leading the country into the abyss , its democracy is doomed . With his violent and poorly conceived policies , the Prime Minister has torn apart a once-cohesive society and undermined Israel 's standing in the U.S. to the point that Americans ' alienation poses a strategic threat . His attempt to use @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Palestinians -- which has been essential to Mr. Netanyahu 's political survival -- only compounds the catastrophe . <p> During the Cold War , the late U.S. diplomat and strategist George Kennan recognized that internal dysfunction and external overreach would cause the Soviet Union to collapse on its own . So , he focused on preventing Soviet expansion while avoiding an unnecessary military showdown . <p> The same containment strategy could have worked against the Islamic Republic , which sooner or later would have collapsed under the weight of its internal contradictions . Instead , the U.S. and Israel initiated a confrontation that was never going to go their way . And whereas the U.S. might be able to absorb the shock of yet another defeat in an asymmetrical war , Israel is no superpower , no matter what Mr. Netanyahu claims .
