@@136651811 <p> Mark Carney 's January speech at Davos -- renouncing what it described as the half-truths of the old liberal order , and explaining his government 's plans to adapt to the new era -- won him much attention . Spanish Prime Minister Pedro S ? nchez 's recent op-ed in Le Monde Diplomatique can be read as a rebuttal to Carney 's position , in defence of the old regime and global institutions . Arranged together , edited snippets of each add up to a kind of debate . <p> Carney : Every morning , this shopkeeper places a sign in his window : ' Workers of the world unite ' . He does n't believe it , no one does , but he places a sign anyway to avoid trouble , to signal compliance , to get along . And because every shopkeeper on every street does the same , the system persists . <p> The system 's power comes not from its truth , but from everyone 's willingness to perform as if it were true , and its fragility comes from the same source . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prospered under what we called the rules-based international order . We knew the story of the international rules-based order was partially false that the strongest would exempt themselves when convenient , that trade rules were enforced asymmetrically . <p> This fiction was useful , and American hegemony , in particular , helped provide public goods , open sea lanes , a stable financial system , collective security and support for frameworks for resolving disputes . <p> So , we placed the sign in the window . <p> This bargain no longer works . <p> S ? nchez : Much of the social world exists only because we collectively agree that it does . A line on a map becomes a border . Words written in a treaty become binding obligations . And yes , a piece of paper becomes wealth . <p> These shared fictions make life in society possible . Money is one of them . So are the multilateral system and the rules of international law that organize relations between states . Yet many who would never question the first are quick to reject the second . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Despite what some claim , the system is not failing people . For the past 75 years it has helped deliver the most prosperous and stable period in human history . This record is far from perfect -- but it is far better than any alternative humanity has ever tried . <h> 2 . Global rules or ? la carte deal making ? <p> Carney cites the various arrangement Canada is attempting with individual nations or small blocs . It 's building coalitions that work -- issues by issue , with partners who share enough common ground to act together . <p> S ? nchez : Today , we need instruments of global governance more than ever . And only the multilateral system can provide that . <p> 3 . Universal educational rights for children are hardly even an afterthought . <p> Winnipeg Free Press Newsletter <h> Jen Zoratti Next <p> Rather than undermine our public schools and their teachers , we should be grateful for what they represent and what they do -- a welcome correct to a world gone mad . Unlike the alternatives , public schools welcome all students regardless of their economic status , their gender identities , their religions and their parents ' politics . Although the challenges can be formidable , attempts are made to make every child feel valued , needed and loved . And unlike the alternatives , public schools must remain unconstrained by religious adherence and partisan loyalty . Public education , simply put , is one of our last hopes for a better @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> In that vein , public school teachers must be allowed , even encouraged , to teach universal humanitarian values , teaching not only tolerance but also mutual acceptance of and respect for difference and diversity . This also means that they need to be able to name and call out inhumanity wherever and whenever it occurs without fear of retribution . Our children deserve no less from those they look to for answers to life 's missteps . For teachers this is not just a calling , it is also a public obligation . <p> The way to make the world a better place is by loving , caring for and educating our children in such a way so , that when adults , they can enjoy the world together with all others . It means that we must honestly and relentlessly confront our political and religious leaders with the devastating consequences of some of their attitudes , behaviours and decisions , demanding that they acknowledge their responsibilities to be examples to our children for how to live good lives contributing to making a better world . We @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , or that it 's just the way we do things now . <p> That is not just an obligation for our teachers , but for all of us who want to be considered adults and democratic citizens ! <p> John R. @@136717325 <h> Record number of antisemitic incidents reported in Canada last year reflect a ' national crisis ' : B'nai Brith <p> It was with " great frustration and disgust , " that B'nai Brith Canada 's director of research and advocacy reported 6,800 antisemitic incidents in Canada last year , at the release of the organization 's annual audit on April 27 . <p> That amounts to 18.6 hateful incidents per day , Richard Robertson told an Ottawa press conference , a 9.3 per cent increase over 2024 , and a stunning 145 per cent hike from 2022 . " It 's a national crisis and is getting worse . " <p> Canada is moving backwards in its fight against racism and hatred said Robertson , lead author of the Annual Audit of Antisemitic Incidents , noting 2025 numbers are " shocking but not surprising , reflecting prolonged inaction , lack of leadership , and if our elected officials do n't respond , civil society must demand a change . " <p> Ontario had almost half of all incidents in 2025 , at 3,194 , an increase of 79.2 per @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cent to 384 ( more than in 2023 and 2024 combined ) ; Manitoba and Saskatchewan collectively saw 841 incidents , an 88.1 per cent increase ; British Columbia had 847 incidents , up 26.2 per cent ; and Yukon , Northwest Territories , and Nunavut recorded 13 events . Quebec ( 573 ) and Alberta ( 677 ) however , saw significant decreases from 1,651 and 916 respectively . <p> From schoolyard slurs , graffiti , and vandalism , to Holocaust denial , death threats and terror plots , last year 's figures mark the highest volume of antisemitic incidents recorded since B'nai Brith 's audits began in 1982 . <p> While linked in part to the Middle East conflict , Robertson said the 2025 antisemitism surge is so widespread it ca n't be attributed to a single cause . " Conspiracies about Jews and Jewish influence in Canada are no longer only something we see on the radical fringes . In recent years , especially 2025 , it has become clear that antisemitism is increasingly normalized throughout Canadian society . " <p> Of the total , 6,491 incidents @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cases of violence . The digital realm remains the primary driver with 91.9 per cent occurring online . <p> Quebec director Paola Samuel noted that " the historic peak in late 2025 after Bondi Beach , when somebody murdered a little girl , and people praying and lighting Hanukkah candles , showed that just when we think that people will rally around us , we are wrong . " <p> To those who ask where the line is for those who want to criticize Israel , Robertson says " that 's not what we recorded in our audit , but rather the demonization of Zionism . " That is , denying the right of Jews to live in their ancestral homeland , and to " subject a minority in this country to unprecedented levels of hate and abuse because of actions of a foreign government . " <p> He added that anti-Zionists often use classic tropes that dehumanize Jews and " when it becomes acceptable , and even popular , to demonize Zionists , Jewish communities suffer . " When Jews ask each other how they 're doing ... we may say ' I 'm great , we 're thriving , ' but it must be qualified . " <p> Wolle called for moral clarity from political leadership , " about what is right and wrong in this country , what it values . We are expecting bold statements without qualifications followed by actions that support those statements . Not word salads . " Calling something a hate crime and then dropping charges , or weak sentencing , " does n't demonstrate seriousness . This is not demonstrating clarity . The fact that we have volunteer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ largely through philanthropy , because our children and families no longer feel safe in this country . <p> " Now not only do they not feel safe , they are not safe , and that has been evidenced by beatings , shootings , people firing into synagogues , schools , businesses , vandalism , threats . Channel 13 had Together and Likud tied at 26 , and Channel 11 had Likud ahead with 27 seats versus Together 's 24 . <p> Hebrew University political scientist Gideon Rahat told The Globe that , traditionally , party mergers prior to an election , in a system that favours a diversity of options , lead to a drop in the polls . <p> " If you take two options , and especially one that is seen as right-wing and the other that 's seen as the centre , and you put them together , you are likely to lose voters , " Prof. Rahat said . <p> Still , it could be effective here given that it 's better to take on Mr. Netanyahu in a one-to-one competition rather than split the votes in a three-way race , he said . <p> This merger , however , was more likely born of pragmatic considerations , such as the fear that despite Yesh Atid 's strong financial and campaign structure , Mr. Lapid 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to survive as a party in the next election , Prof. Rahat explained . Meanwhile , Mr. Bennett 's party , Bennett 2026 , has seen its support climb but lacked the necessary funds for the campaign . <p> Pollster and former Netanyahu adviser Mitchell Barak told The Globe the merger is good for Mr. Netanyahu because it demonstrates he is still so powerful that his opponents figured it would take a union of two veteran politicians to oust him . <p> Mr. Bennett has a history of starting out strong but petering out , while Mr. Netanyahu is often initially weak but ends up on top , Mr. Barak explained . <p> " He starts at a low point , it looks like he is going to lose , and then he rallies the troops , " he said . <p> Mr. Bushinsky said the critical question , though , is not which party gets the most votes but which one can form a coalition of 61 seats in the 120-seat Knesset . <p> Polls conducted Monday show that the Bennett-Lapid alliance will not be able to form a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , on the Arab-Israeli party Ra'am , headed by Mansour Abbas , which is expected to win five seats . <p> It 's believed the presence of Ra'am in the 2021 coalition was partly responsible for its demise . <p> Based on the latest polls , the opposition bloc led by Together would have 60 seats without Ra'am , while the government bloc would have 50 . The Arab parties , which are currently in neither bloc , make up 10 seats . <p> Unless something changes , Mr. Bushinsky said , Israel could face the same stalemate that plagued it in 2019 and 2020 , when it went from election to election without a clear winner . <p> Among the factors that could influence the outcome of the election is the war with Iran , for which a fragile ceasefire is in place . <p> " So , for example , should the Islamic Republic in Iran fall , that would likely hand Netanyahu an electoral victory . " Amid that kind of craziness , there 's probably no point observing that separation of church and state is constitutionally enshrined , nor that a third of Americans are either not Christian or non-religious . <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> Meanwhile , Vice-President JD Vance , a convert to Catholicism , is proselytizing with a new book called " Communion " and lecturing the Pope about staying in his own lane . " Exactly . <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> No matter its name , religion usually embraces three elements : faith in a divinity , rites and rituals honouring that faith , and an inviolable moral code . <p> Such things light the way for a lot of good in this dark world , most of it done quietly , away from the headlines . Marginalized people are fed and cared for , the distressed are comforted , souls searching for meaning are offered purpose and hope . And the gift of religious inspiration in the arts is available to everyone . You do n't have to believe in God to feel the yearning human spirit in Mozart 's " Ave Verum . " <p> And yet the soul of all this is abased and abused by con artists twisting religion to fit insufferable egos and despicable political ends . <p> Committing terrible @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to all people of good will , whether or not they profess religious faith , because it 's hypocritical , cynical and profoundly immoral . <p> That , in religious terms , is really the ultimate blasphemy . <p> Janice Kennedy is a retired journalist and contributing Star columnist . During the year , Arnot and his colleagues on the Standing Senate Committee on Human Rights heard 44 witnesses over seven meetings . They also received 36 written briefs . It was n't nearly as extensive a study as one done two years ago on Islamophobia , and the committee had until the end @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his colleagues felt the " clear and present danger " of antisemitism was so serious , they wanted their recommendations for action to come out immediately . <p> " We 're not asking for the government to sort of dither around and think about it . @@136751720 <p> Thornhill 's Beth Avraham Yoseph of Toronto ( BAYT ) synagogue on Saturday morning , March 7 , 2026 , after bullets were fired overnight at BAYT and at Shaarei Shomayim in Toronto , within about 30 minutes of one another . ( Credit : Ellin Bessner ) <p> The security of Canadian places of worship , schools , community centres , and other institutions received a big boost in Tuesday 's Spring Economic Statement . <p> As part of its $36 billion in new spending envelope , Ottawa announced $75 million in additional funding to Public Safety Canada over five years starting in 2026-27 , for the Canada Community Security Program ( CCSP ) , which offers grants to communities to strengthen security measures at vulnerable spaces , including infrastructure upgrades , security guard funding , and emergency training supports . <p> In a statement , the federal government attributed that need to " a significant increase in police-reported hate crime in recent years , including incidents driven by Islamophobia and antisemitism and hate targeting LGBTQ+ communities . " While all communities have access , Canadian Jews remain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Canada data , and recent reports by B'nai Brith Canada and the Senate Committee on Human Rights . <p> The additional cash will allow the program to continue and will be front-loaded , with $35 million available in this coming fiscal year , followed by $30 million , and $23 million annually going forward . <p> " That reflects the acuity of the situation we 're facing , " Centre for Israel and Jewish Affairs ( CIJA ) CEO Noah Shack told The CJN . " We need more resources now in order to get things built and reinforce projects that are already underway . " <p> CIJA ? led the charge to boost funding and welcomed ? the news , Shack calling the government 's more than doubling its funding allocation over five years a significant step forward in safeguarding Canada 's Jewish community . Since the Hamas attack on Israel on Oct. 7 , 2023 and the subsequent war in Gaza , threats targeting Canadian Jews have continued to escalate , he said . Canada 's security agencies have warned that a violent attack against the Jewish community @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's essential that government programs evolve to meet this challenge head-on . " <p> The CCSP covers private , not-for-profit organizations like educational institutions , shelters for victims of gender-based violence , places of worship , community centres , office and administrative spaces , camps , cemeteries , and childcare centres , with up to 70 per cent of eligible project expenses . <p> The new funds for the program , which is open to all Canadians , along with the recent $10 million in emergency funding announced for Jewish community security , represent Canada 's most substantial investment in community security to date , investments that advocates say are essential for the future of Jewish life in Canada . <p> From bollards and cameras to fences and personnel , the annual costs of keeping the Jewish community safe have soared to some $100 million across Canada , according to CIJA and Canada 's Jewish federations which wrote to Prime Minister Mark Carney ahead of his government 's Spring Economic Statement . Every dollar spent on metal detectors , security cameras and security guards is a dollar less spent on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and seniors , and other aspects of maintaining Jewish life in Canada . <p> What more , says Shack , he and federation leaders also told Carney in their meeting at the start of the fall parliamentary session that " this was the community 's top priority , reminding him of his own party 's election campaign statement that increased security costs amidst rising antisemitism should not be borne by the Jewish community alone . " <p> There are also program changes spurred by recommendations from the community , namely " to empower community security agencies to distribute funds directly to the community . There are a number of pilot projects that we were able to get off the ground over the last year that enabled either bulk purchasing or the administration of grants themselves . " <p> That means faster deployment , a more seamless integration and unified approach . " In addition , we 've been talking about the need for some operational costs that are incurred , and the infrastructure that 's required , and creating more flexibility in the system to maximize support to communities at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ impactfully as possible . " <p> Along with new money comes changes in how it 's managed , as in the past , communities have been plagued by significant delays to get a grant approved . " There were situations where ( a synagogue or centre ) would finally get approval to build a fence , but it was the middle of winter and they had to build it immediately or they would lose the grant , just those sorts of idiosyncrasies that should n't be happening , given the stakes . " <p> Money for security notwithstanding , government and law enforcement need to focus on dealing with the root causes of the threats , " countering the foreign interference fuelling radicalization , strengthening measures against the promotion of terrorism , and ensuring that existing laws are consistently enforced and offenders held to account . <p> " The solution is not just to build higher walls , " Shack insists , " but if we do n't build those walls in the immediate term , we wo n't be in a position to effectively fight against those forces . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , but ultimately , it 's a threat to our Canadian way of life , and we need to protect ourselves in the immediate term while being fully committed to pushing back against those forces . " <p> The arc of government spending on the program has been a large one : before the October 2023 terrorist attack , the CCSP and its predecessor , the Security Infrastructure Program , offered limited funding , less than $10 million , while in the coming year $45 million will be available , $10 million of it directly to the Jewish community . <p> Shack lauded " incredible unity in the community throughout this effort " as the prime minister and members of Parliament across Canada received " tens of thousands of calls and emails from concerned Jewish Canadians " and other communities , he told The CJN . " Hundreds of Jewish organizations joined together to write to the prime minister . Meetings were held across Canada to share what Jewish life is like right now and the threats being faced , and all of these efforts had impact . It was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> CIJA noted that Canada 's per capita investment in Jewish community security has remained one-third that of the United Kingdom and one-quarter that of Australia . UK government security funding to protect Jewish schools , synagogues , and community centres with guards , CCTV , alarms , and physical protection is administered primarily through the Community Security Trust , which receives some $33.5 million annually , plus a $100 million package until 2028 , with additional emergency funding in response to threats against the UK Jewish population of some 300,000 , about two-thirds the size of Canada 's . <p> And real threats are being signalled . According to a March report from the federal Integrated Threat Assessment Centre ( ITAC ) comprised of professionals from the Canadian Security Intelligence Service , Canada Border Services , Department of National Defence , and the RCMP , Jewish schools , community centres , and synagogues face a heightened risk , with the most probable scenario involving a single individual attacking targets that are easy to access using simple means such as a knife , firearm , or vehicle . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is now seen patrolling around synagogues and community centres , but also at public venues like sporting events and tourist attractions . No such measures have been announced or seen in other Canadian cities . <p> " While institutions need protection , the broader threat affects society as a whole , " says Shack . " Kids in daycare , people going to pray , visiting loved ones in seniors ' homes , these are everyday activities requiring protection right now . " <p> The ICC , located in the Hague , Netherlands , is an intergovernmental body that prosecutes international crimes . In 2024 , the ICC put out arrest warrants for Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his former defense minister on charges of war crimes and crimes against humanity -- charges they both deny . @@136786241 <h> Opinion : Stop dismissing Jewish safety concerns on campus <p> Protestors outside Dawson College in Montreal on March 26 , 2025 . ( Photos by Joel Ceausu ) <p> Much has been made of Jewish students ' claims not to feel safe on Canadian campuses . Professors and press alike have regarded these concerns as a ' feeling ' , not reality , or even as an expression of deserved discomfort . Others believe these worries have been weaponized against pro-Palestinian voices . In a talk at the Vanier College Social Sciences Festival in October 2024 , Professor Anna Zalik , steering group member of the Jewish Faculty Network group supporting Palestine , referred to the use of " language of safety to prevent open discussion . " Similarly , in response to a government investigation into the tense climate at two Montreal-area Cegeps , reported on in The CJN , my local union took the position that discussion of Jewish safety is often conflated with the comfort of Jewish students . It stated that discomfort from political discourse is not the same as a threat to safety . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to get comfortable with being uncomfortable . <p> There is an interesting argument to be considered in this viewpoint . Higher education is , after all , supposed to be a place for examining one 's beliefs and prior knowledge . It would make sense , then , that Jewish students who have n't been exposed to much critique of Israel might be shifting in their seats a little during the discussion of current events . Professors who believe Jewish students are just uncomfortable , like the mysterious composers of my union 's position document , might be surprised to discover that almost everyone does not disagree that Jewish students having to confront different views on Israel . That , however , is not what Jewish students mean when they say they feel unsafe . While threats to physical safety have ( so far ) been relatively rare , though not non-existent , Jewish students are , for the most part , concerned with their psychological safety . <p> What exactly is psychological safety ? It has been defined in numerousplaces as the ability to take risks and express oneself @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ apt to feel psychologically safe to express their opinions about the Middle East when their teacher 's choice of course materials are entirely one sided ? Or how about when the course text is by a former spokesman of the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine , a designated terrorist organization according to Canada and many of our allies ? Or when the only student in the class wearing a kippah is chosen to play the role of the " banker " during a classroom simulation of the " occupation " of Palestine . Or when their teachers link assignments to attendance at a talk explaining how Israel 's " pinkwashing " of its political violence harms queer Palestinians and that the best solution for all Palestinians would be the return of the Palestine Liberation Organization . If these examples that have been shared with me by students in Montreal colleges are n't enough , consider the McGill students whose class was disrupted by masked student strikers on November 17 while the professor looked on passively . Or , more notably , think about William Clare Roberts who took @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support " of Hezbollah and Hamas . I 'm sure Jewish students feel free to disagree openly in his class . <p> Having established that Jewish students might just have a point about their psychological safety ( surrounding their social standing and treatment by their teachers including in their grades ) in the face of unrelating and one-sided focus on Israel in their institutions , let 's consider the voices dismissing these concerns . Before Oct. 7 , I was told by a colleague that things are n't so bad for Jews in Quebec because " no one has been shot . " My colleague was , of course , making a comparison to the Quebec City mosque shooting of 2017 . In this view , only so-called " racialized " and " minoritized " groups can be harmed . Similar to the understanding that only a massacre of Holocaust proportions can actually cause damage to Jewish communities , unless Jews in Canada face lethal violence , the thinking goes , they do not deserve minority status . <p> Oct. 7 and the ensuing war entrenched this stance in academia @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ racialized minority " but most of them support the so-called " genocider state . " It is believed to be a social imperative , rather than a type of harm , to call out ( a pre-supposed ) genocide , but is , rather , a social imperative . So said a teacher from a Montreal college speaking into a megaphone at one of the 2024 strikes for Gaza : " ' There is no such thing as neutrality . ' " Her students surely feel completely safe to express opposing views in her classes . <p> In its position against the government investigation , my union referenced a piece in The Conversation by two Canadian academics . Natalie Kouri-Towe 's 2024 publication , based on her expertise in trigger warnings , covers topics like " gender inclusive language in classrooms " and addressing racial violence in education . " These are the kinds of " strategies " to which she and Matthews are referring when they say that academics need to " hold space for differences , even when they challenge our understanding of the world . " But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ development of an almost completely homogeneous environment within our institutions of higher education . Students and teachers might look different , but they do n't think different . And those who do risk social ostracization and academic penalties . <p> Ultimately , casting the question as one of Jewish comfort rather than safety engages two fallacies . First , it casts Jews as the same by presupposing wrongdoing on the part of Jewish communities everywhere and delighting in the comeuppance they are receiving as a group of oppressor whites who have too long benefitted from the comfort provided by their exploitation of racialized minorities . Second , it projects Jews as different -- whiny complainers who are always manipulating standards to extract extra care , grades and attention . Both of these views are stereotypes that do not take the actual concerns of Jewish students about the real threats to their psychological safety into consideration . <p> Toby Moneit is a teacher at a public college in Montreal . <p> *** <p> What do you think ? The CJN welcomes your feedback with a letter to the editor .
